--- Model Response ---
To determine the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes (B and H), we need to analyze the graph's structure and identify all paths between these nodes.

1. **Identify the black nodes**: The black nodes are B and H.
2. **Identify the immediate neighboring nodes**:
   - B's immediate neighbors are A, E, J, S, T, and C.
   - H's immediate neighbors are J, I, and N.

3. **Trace the paths between B and H**:
   - From B, it connects to J, which also connects to H.
   - From B, it connects to E, A, S, and C, but these do not directly connect to H.
   - From B, it connects to T, which connects to C, G, and Q. However, C connects to H via I, while G and Q do not directly connect to H.
   - From H, it connects to I, which connects to Q, R, and G. Q, R, and G do not directly connect to B.
   - From H, it connects to N, which does not connect to B.

4. **Identify the bottleneck**:
   - The paths from B to H are primarily through J and H.
   - Therefore, removing the edge (B, J) and (H, J) will disconnect B and H.

5. **Verify the minimum number of edges to remove**:
   - Removing both edges (B, J) and (H, J) ensures that there is no path between B and H.

Thus, the minimum number of edges that must be removed is 2.

**Answer:**

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(B, J), (H, J)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'G']]}

   - F is connected to S, S is connected to N, N is connected to O, O is connected to G, G is connected to H, H is connected to J, and J is connected to C. This forms a longer path: F → S → N → O → G → H → J → C.

4. **Determine the minimum set of edges to remove** to disconnect the paths:
   - The first and shortest path can be broken by removing either (F, T) or (T, C).
   - The second path can be broken by removing either (S, N), (N, O), (O, G), (G, H), (H, J), or (J, C).

   Since we need the minimum number of edges to remove, we can focus on the shortest paths. Removing edge (F, T) or (T, C) breaks the shortest path, but since both endpoints connect directly, we do not need to remove both. For the longer path, removing any single edge (S, N), (N, O), (O, G), (G, H), (H, J), or (J, C) would suffice. However, since there is an overlap in the intersection of path, and we need to ensure the longer path does not overlap with the shorter one, we need to choose the edge which does not affect the shorter path. Therefore, the minimal set involves only removing (F, T).

5. **Verify the minimal edges remove** result in complete disconnection.
   - By removing (F, T), we successfully prevent both the direct and indirect paths between F and C. There are no remaining paths that connect them.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes (F and C) is:

--- Model Response ---

1. **Identify the black nodes:** The two black nodes are R and J.
2. **Examine the immediate neighboring nodes:**
   - The immediate neighboring nodes of R are B, A, and F.
   - The immediate neighboring nodes of J are H, Q, and I.

3. **Iteratively find other neighboring nodes:**
   - Neighbors of B: S, E, T, N, C, H, and A.
   - Neighbors of A: N and C.
   - Neighbors of F: S, T, and the path continues on.
   - Neighbors of H: B, I, P.
   - Neighbors of Q: I, P, and J.
   - Neighbors of I: H, P, Q.

4. **Identify the paths connecting R and J:**
   - Path: R → B → H → J.
   - Path: R → B → C → H → J.
   - Path: R → B → I → J.

5. **Determine the bottleneck (common edges):**
   - All paths from R to J must pass through either B or H.

6. **Evaluate the edges to be removed:**
   - Removing the edge (B, H) alone might not disconnect R and J completely because they might still connect through other paths involving C or I.
   - To completely disconnect R from J, we need to remove the edges (B, H) and (B, I).

7. **Check the minimal set:**
   - Removing (B, H) and (B, I) ensures that no path exists between R and J.

Thus, the minimum number of edges to be removed is 2:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(B, H), (B, I)].

1. **Identify the black nodes:**
   - Black nodes: A and T.

2. **Identify the immediate neighboring nodes of the black nodes:**

   - **Neighbors of A**: B, N, C
   - **Neighbors of T**: B, F

3. **Identify secondary or higher-order neighboring nodes that connect the paths:**

   - From A to B, then B to T. So, A -> B -> T is one path.
   - From A to N, then N to O, then O to G, then G to H, then H to P, then P to Q, then Q to I, then I to J, then J to R, and R to B, finally to T. So, A -> N -> O -> G -> H -> P -> Q -> I -> J -> R -> B -> T is another path.
   - From A to N, then N to C, then C to G, then G to H, then H to B, then B to T. So, A -> N -> C -> G -> H -> B -> T is another path.
   - From A to C, then C to G, then G to H, then H to P, then P to Q, then Q to I, then I to J, then J to R, and R to B, finally to T. So, A -> C -> G -> H -> P -> Q -> I -> J -> R -> B -> T is another path.
   - From A to C, then C to G, then G to H, then H to B, then B to T. This results in a shorter path: A -> C -> G -> H -> B -> T.

4. **Evaluate Bottlenecks:**

   We observe that many paths necessarily pass through nodes B, H, and G, creating potential bottlenecks.

5. **Determine which edges must be removed:**

   Each path needs to cross either B, H, or G. Checking each:

   - Removing the edge (B, H) will cut off paths from A to B to T directly.
   - The remaining paths except those that can go via node G, can be independently further cut by removing (H, G).

6. **Check minimal set combinations:**

   To minimise the number of edges removed, the combination of (B, H) and (H, G) is minimal.

Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(B, H), (H, G)]
